--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Let me analyze the mission carefully:

**Scoring rule:**
- Before switch time: score += 1 per **stationary** robot each step
- After switch time: score += 1 per **moving** robot each step

**Optimal strategy:**
- Before switch time: ALL robots should **move** (to avoid penalty)
- After switch time: ALL robots should **stop** (to avoid penalty)

**Key perception:** At switch time, one wall block lights up **GREEN**. This is the signal robots can use to detect the phase change!

**Ideal FSM:**
- State 0: Move/Explore (before switch - avoid stationary penalty)
- State 1: Stop (after switch - avoid moving penalty)
- Transition: State 0 → State 1 when GREEN color is perceived (C=7, l=2 for green)

The champion (all-Stop, score 10032) is terrible because robots are stationary the whole time, accumulating penalty before switch time. With ~500 steps before switch and 20 robots, that's ~10000 penalty - which matches!

The ideal would be: move before switch (~500 steps × 0 stationary penalty), stop after switch (~700 steps × 0 moving penalty) = ~0 score.

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion (elitism)
2. Main approach: Explore → Stop on seeing green (C=7, l=2)
3. Variants with different probabilities for the green detection
4. Some with exploration + fallback transitions
5. Some 3-state machines for robustness

The green color is 2 (green=2 in the color list based on typical ARGoS conventions, but let me check: {1,2,3,4,5,6} - likely 1=red,2=green,3=blue,4=yellow,5=magenta,6=cyan... but the problem says clr options are {1,2,3,4,5,6}. The wall lights up GREEN, so I'll use clr=2 for green detection).

Wait, re-reading: `--l<I>x<K>` for C=7 uses {1,2,3,4,5,6}. Green should be 2.

Let me design the machines:

- **FSM1 (main):** State0=Explore → Stop when see green (p=0.9)
- **FSM2:** State0=Explore → Stop when see green (p=1.0)  
- **FSM3:** State0=Explore (high turn) → Stop when see green (p=0.8)
- **FSM4:** State0=Explore → Stop when see green, also random stop (p=0.001)
- **FSM5:** 3-state: Explore → GoToColor(green) → Stop
- **FSM6:** State0=Explore with attraction, → Stop on green
- Various other combinations

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion uses:
- State 0: Explore (rwm=10) → transition to Stop when seeing green (p=1.0) OR random transition (p=0.002)
- State 1: Stop → random transition back to Explore (p=0.001)

Score is 7445.52, much better than the all-stop champion (10032). The green detection is working!

**Analysis:**
- The small random probability (p=0.002) from State 0 → Stop means some robots stop too early (before seeing green)
- The p=0.001 from Stop → Explore means occasionally robots start moving again after stopping
- This is close to ideal but not perfect

**What could improve it:**
1. Remove or reduce the random transition from State 0 to Stop (p=0.002 causes premature stopping)
2. Keep the green detection as primary trigger
3. The back-transition from Stop (p=0.001) might actually help if a robot incorrectly stopped before the switch
4. Try higher exploration rates so more robots can see the green wall
5. Consider attraction behavior to spread robots to see the green wall better
6. Try GoToColor(green) as intermediate state to get closer to green wall before stopping

**Key insight:** The random p=0.002 transition from Explore→Stop is problematic. It means on average every 500 steps a robot stops randomly - which could be before the switch time, adding to the pre-switch penalty.

Let me design better machines:
- Remove random early-stop transition
- Try different rwm values (higher = more exploration = more likely to face green wall)
- Try 3-state: Explore → GoToColor(green) → Stop
- Try having the back-transition from Stop be on NOT seeing green (but that's not directly available)

Note: LED colors allowed are {0,4,5,6} only (0=off, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan). Green (2) is NOT allowed for LEDs!

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion hasn't improved from round 2 (still 7445.52). Let me think more carefully about why.

**Score analysis:**
- With 20 robots, 500 pre-switch steps where all move = 0 penalty before switch
- With 700 post-switch steps where all stop = 0 penalty after switch
- Theoretical minimum = 0

**Champion behavior:**
- State 0: Explore (rwm=10) → Stop when seeing green (p=1.0) OR random stop (p=0.002)
- State 1: Stop → random return to Explore (p=0.001)

**Problem:** 7445 score means significant penalty accumulating. The issue is:
1. Robots that never see the green wall keep exploring after switch (getting penalized)
2. The p=0.002 random stop causes some robots to stop before switch (getting penalized)
3. Not all robots can see the green wall from their position

**Key insight I missed:** Robots can communicate! When a robot detects green and stops, other robots could detect that stopped cluster and also stop. This would help robots that can't see the green wall directly.

**Better strategy:**
- State 0: Explore (moving before switch - good)
- State 1: Stop (after switch - good)  
- Transition: Explore → Stop when seeing green (primary) OR when many neighbors are stopped (neighbors below threshold - but we can't directly detect stopped robots)

Actually, we can use neighbor count: if robots cluster near the green wall and stop, others might see MORE neighbors (attraction effect) or use the LED signaling.

Let me try: robots in Stop state emit a colored LED (yellow=4), then exploring robots transition to Stop when they see yellow (LED color 4).

**3-state cascade approach:**
- State 0: Explore → Stop when see green (direct) OR when see yellow LED (indirect from other robots)
- State 1: Stop, emit yellow LED → rarely go back

Also: maybe the issue is rwm=10 is too low. Higher rwm = more random turning = robots face more directions = more likely to see green wall.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Huge improvement! Score dropped from 7445 to 1933. The key insight that worked:

**Champion:**
- State 0: Explore (rwm=10) → Stop when seeing GREEN (p=1.0) OR when seeing YELLOW LED (p=0.9, from stopped robots)
- State 1: Stop, emit YELLOW LED → random return to Explore (p=0.001)

This creates a cascade: first robot sees green wall → stops and emits yellow → nearby robots see yellow → also stop and emit yellow → cascade spreads.

**Why 1933 and not lower?**
- Some robots never see green OR yellow from stopped robots
- The p=0.9 for yellow detection might miss some robots
- rwm=10 might not be optimal for coverage
- The p=0.001 back-transition means some robots occasionally restart moving after switch

**Improvements to try:**
1. Increase yellow detection probability to 1.0 (or keep 0.9 but add more transitions)
2. Try different rwm values (higher = more random turning = better coverage)
3. Add a neighbor-count condition: if many neighbors stopped (high neighbor count near green wall), stop too
4. Try magenta (5) or cyan (6) LED signals instead
5. Try 3-state: Explore → GoToColor(green) → Stop with yellow signaling
6. Reduce back-transition probability even more (0.0001)
7. Add both green AND yellow detection with p=1.0

Key variants to explore:
- Same topology but p=1.0 for yellow detection
- Same topology but different rwm values (20, 30, 50)
- Add neighbor count as additional trigger (many neighbors = cluster of stopped robots)
- 3-state with GoToColor intermediate

################################################################################
# ROUND 6/10
################################################################################

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Score is stuck at 1933.34. The champion hasn't improved across rounds 4 and 5. Let me think more carefully.

**Champion analysis:**
- State 0: Explore (rwm=10) → Stop when see GREEN (p=1.0) OR when see YELLOW (p=0.9)
- State 1: Stop, emit YELLOW → random return to Explore (p=0.001)

**Why 1933 and not lower?** 
With 20 robots, 1200 steps, switch ~500, theoretical minimum approaches 0. Score 1933 suggests significant remaining penalty. 

**What's still going wrong:**
1. Before switch: robots are moving (good) but some may stop prematurely if they see yellow from other robots that also stopped prematurely (false cascade)
2. After switch: robots that never encounter yellow or green keep moving (bad)
3. The cascade only spreads within 0.5m range - robots far from stopped clusters never get the signal

**Key problem:** The yellow signal can only spread 0.5m. If robots are spread across a large arena, the cascade may not reach all of them before the run ends.

**Solutions to try:**
1. **Repulsion before switch:** Keep robots spread to avoid false yellow cascades, but still respond to green/yellow
2. **Different LED colors:** Use different colors to distinguish green-wall-detected vs yellow-cascade
3. **Higher rwm:** More random turning increases chance of seeing green wall early
4. **GoToColor approach:** Once a robot sees yellow, go toward yellow (clustering), then stop
5. **Neighbor count trigger:** Stop if many neighbors nearby (implies cluster of stopped robots)
6. **Reduce false positives:** Maybe p=0.9 for yellow is too high - false yellow detections before switch?

Wait - can robots see yellow LEDs from OTHER robots? Yes! The camera detects colored objects including robot LEDs. So if any robot accidentally emits yellow before switch, it triggers others. But State 1 emits yellow and has p=0.001 to return to State 0... so if a robot goes to State 1 prematurely, it emits yellow and cascades others.

**The p=0.001 back-transition is actually important** - it means premature-stopped robots eventually return to exploring.

**New ideas:**
- Use GoToColor(yellow) instead of just detecting yellow - approach stopped cluster, then join it
- 3-state: Explore → GoToColor(yellow/green) → Stop
- Use attraction to cluster near stopped robots

# ROUND 7/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

New champion: Score 1200.05! The key change was using **Repulsion** (B=5, rep=2) instead of Exploration in State 0. This makes sense:

**Why Repulsion works better:**
- Robots repel each other → they spread out across the arena
- More spread = higher chance that at least one robot is near the green wall when it lights up
- Better coverage also means yellow LED signals from stopped robots can reach more robots (more distributed cascade)
- Repulsion keeps robots moving (good before switch) while maximizing coverage

**Current champion:**
- State 0: Repulsion (rep=2) → Stop when see GREEN (p=1.0) OR YELLOW (p=0.9)
- State 1: Stop, emit YELLOW → random return to Repulsion (p=0.001)

**Score 1200 analysis:**
- ~1200 penalty remaining. With 20 robots, this could be ~60 robots-steps equivalent, suggesting a few robots still miss the signal
- The cascade might not reach robots that are far from any stopped robot

**Improvements:**
1. Try different repulsion gains (1.0, 3.0, 4.0, 5.0)
2. Try higher yellow detection probability (1.0 instead of 0.9)
3. Try lower back-transition probability (0.0001)
4. Try 3-state: Repulsion → GoToColor(yellow) → Stop (approach stopped cluster)
5. Add neighbor count as additional trigger
6. Try combining repulsion with different LED signals

The score 1200 is very close to 1200 steps × 1 robot = the score of just 1 robot moving the whole time after switch, or similarly 1 robot stationary the whole time before switch. So we're nearly perfect but a few robots still behave suboptimally.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Score improved to 1076.85 with rep=5 (maximum repulsion). This confirms that stronger repulsion = better spread = better coverage of the arena = more robots near the green wall when it lights up.

**Champion:** Repulsion(rep=5) → Stop when see GREEN(p=1) or YELLOW(p=0.9); Stop emits YELLOW → rarely restart(p=0.001)

**Why ~1076 remaining?**
- 1076 / 20 robots ≈ 54 steps per robot of wrong behavior
- Some robots still fail to get the signal (green or yellow cascade)
- The arena might be large enough that yellow cascade (0.5m range) doesn't propagate fast enough to all robots

**Key remaining issues:**
1. Yellow cascade propagation is limited by 0.5m range - robots far from any stopped robot never hear it
2. p=0.9 for yellow: maybe 1.0 is better (no missed detections)
3. The p=0.001 back-transition: maybe too high - robots that correctly stopped might restart
4. Could try adding more LED colors for cascade (multiple signals)
5. Could try GoToColor approach when yellow detected - approach the cluster, then stop

**New explorations:**
1. rep=5, p_yellow=1.0 (no misses on yellow)
2. rep=5, p=0.001 → 0.0001 (less restart)
3. 3-state: Repulsion → GoToColor(yellow=4) → Stop (physically approach stopped cluster)
4. rep=5 + also detect magenta/cyan for secondary cascade
5. Try rep=5 with neighbor-count trigger (many neighbors = must be near cluster)
6. Try different LED colors for the signal (cyan=6 or magenta=5)

The GoToColor(yellow) intermediate state is interesting: when a robot sees yellow, instead of immediately stopping, it moves toward the yellow source. This would physically cluster robots near the stopped group, and then they stop when close enough.

Wait - but GoToColor needs clr parameter. Yellow in the color list... looking at the behaviors: `--clr<I> one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}`. The LED colors are 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan. For GoToColor, these same numbers should refer to the same colors.

## Reasoning

Score improved to 1007.44! The champion now uses:
- State 0: Repulsion(rep=5) → Stop when see GREEN(p=1.0) OR YELLOW(p=1.0)
- State 1: Stop, emit YELLOW → random restart (p=0.0001, very rare)

Key changes that helped: p_yellow=1.0 (no missed yellow detections) and p_restart=0.0001 (very rarely restarts).

**Why ~1007 remaining?**
1007 / 1200 steps ≈ 0.84, and with 20 robots, that's about 1007/20 ≈ 50 steps per robot of wrong behavior. 

The score is getting close to a floor. Let me think about what's still causing penalty:
- Before switch (~500 steps): robots are repelling (moving) ✓ good
- After switch: robots that haven't seen green or yellow yet are still moving ✗ bad

The cascade can only propagate at 0.5m range. If a robot is isolated (no neighbors within 0.5m), it never receives the yellow signal. With strong repulsion (rep=5), robots spread out, which is good for coverage but may make them TOO isolated for cascade propagation.

**Trade-off:** High repulsion = good coverage (more likely to see green) but poor cascade (isolated robots miss yellow signal).

**Ideas to try:**
1. After seeing yellow, move toward yellow source (GoToColor yellow) before stopping - helps cascade propagate
2. Try slightly lower repulsion (rep=3 or 4) - less spread but better cascade
3. Add neighbor count condition: if 0 neighbors (isolated), do something different
4. Try emitting BOTH yellow and another color for wider detection
5. 3-state: Repulsion → GoToColor(yellow/green) → Stop with yellow

The GoToColor(yellow) intermediate is promising: when a robot sees yellow, it approaches the stopped cluster, gets within range, stops and emits yellow itself, creating a growing cascade.

Also: maybe detect color 2 (green) from wall AND also from robot LEDs? No - robots can't emit green (not in {0,4,5,6}).

Let me try the GoToColor intermediate approach more carefully, and also fine-tune the rep gain.

## Reasoning

Score barely improved (1007.44 → 1001.58). We're stuck near ~1000. This is the final round, so let me think carefully about what's happening and what bold changes could break through.

**Current champion analysis:**
- State 0: Repulsion(rep=5) → Stop when see GREEN(p=1) OR YELLOW(p=1)
- State 1: Stop, emit YELLOW → random restart (p=0.0001)

**Why ~1000 score floor?**
- With switch time uniform in [400,600] and 20 robots, 1000 penalty ≈ some robots still moving after switch
- Score ≈ 1000 could be: ~2 robots moving for the entire post-switch period (700 steps × 2 = 1400... too high)
- More likely: several robots moving for ~50 steps each before getting the cascade signal

**The fundamental problem:** Robots with rep=5 are MAXIMALLY spread. The yellow cascade has 0.5m range. If robots are spread across a large arena, an isolated robot may never receive the yellow signal within the post-switch period.

**What could genuinely help:**
1. **GoToColor(yellow) intermediate**: When a robot sees yellow, move toward it (closing the gap), then stop. This brings isolated robots into range.
2. **Emit yellow DURING repulsion phase too** (cle0=4): Then ALL moving robots emit yellow, but wait - this means robots see yellow before switch and stop prematurely! Bad.
3. **Different cascade mechanism**: Use neighbor count - if many robots nearby (cluster), stop. But with repulsion, few neighbors...
4. **Reduced repulsion after switch signal**: 3-state: Repulsion → Attraction(toward yellow) → Stop
5. **Multiple LED colors**: Use both yellow and cyan signals - robots detect either
6. **Exploration instead of repulsion**: With rwm=100 (max turning), robots cover more ground?

The GoToColor(yellow) approach seems most promising for the cascade problem. Let me try it carefully:
- State 0: Repulsion → if see GREEN → go to State 2(Stop); if see YELLOW → go to State 1(GoToColor yellow)
- State 1: GoToColor(yellow), emit yellow → if see yellow very close (p=1) → Stop
- State 2: Stop, emit yellow

Actually the 3-state GoToColor intermediate is the key new idea. Also let me try emitting yellow from the GoToColor state too, so the cascade propagates while robots are moving toward it.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
